### Validation of market incomes

It should be kept in mind that there are several sources providing data
about the number of employed and self-employed (LFS; National Accounts;
National Social Security Institute (NSSI): Statistics on insured
persons). To maintain consistency, we validated the number of recipients
of employment and self-employment income as well as the aggregate
amounts of these income sources in EU-SILC against data from National
Accounts (see Table A3.1 and Table A3.2 in Annex 3).

Table A3.1 shows that the **number of individuals with taxable
employment income** (EUROMOD variable name for employment income is
yemtx) in the EU-SILC survey for 2022 is 27 percentage points (pp)
higher than the number provided by the National Accounts data, while
2023 suggests 22 pp higher value in comparison to 23 pp higher value in
2024. This could be explained mainly by unofficial (“shadow economy”)
employment providing incomes that are not reported to the NSSI and
National Revenue Agency (NRA).[^3] In contrast, Table A3.2 shows that in
2022 the taxable employment income (yemtx) in EU-SILC is close in line
with the National Accounts figure for “Wages and Salaries”, just 4 pp
lower, but understating it in 2023 and 2024 by 6 pp and 5 pp,
respectively. It should be noted though that, in addition to gross
wages/salaries, the National Accounts data include also in-kind payments
which in EU-SILC are reported separately from employment income.

In terms of the taxable **self-employment income** (ysetx), Table A3.1
shows that the number of self-employed individuals in EU-SILC is 150%
(in 2022) of the National Accounts figures, rising to 197% (in 2023) and
slightly declining to 184% (in 2024) of the National Accounts figures.
The reason for this could be under-representation of entrepreneurs in
the external source, particularly those in “A- Agriculture, Forestry and
Fishing” sector. The divergence is even stronger for the amounts of
S**elf-employed social insurance contributions**, where the baseline
amounts are almost three to four times higher than external values, with
ratios increasing from **2.90 in 2022** to **3.61 in 2023** and
remaining elevated at **3.58 in 2024**.

**Investment income** (yiy) was found not to be feasible for validation,
as the recording of such sources in surveys is usually not reliable.
Similarly, the income from shares and other securities, e.g. the
dividend income, is not recorded consistently in the National Accounts
or in the survey data. We could not find information from external
sources on the number of recipients/payers and total amount of **private
transfers** (paid to and received from other households). Even though
**private pension** receivers are rarely met in the population, the
EU-SILC 2022 survey underestimates the number of recipients by 90 pp in
2022 compared to the external figure, but in 2022 and 2023 the number of
private pensions receivers is underestimated by hefty 91 pp and by 97
pp, respectively. Nevertheless, the total amount of private pensions in
the EU-SILC understates the external figure by 66 pp for 20221 and
understates by 85 pp the 2023 figure and by 91 pp the 2024 figure. In
late 2021 it is the first time retirees start receiving (were eligible)
for private pensions and there is an option to get the individually
accumulated private pension funds as a lump sum as well, which further
complicates the process of matching external data with survey or
modelled data.

### Validation of taxes and social insurance contributions

In this subsection, we validate the results from the policy simulations
of SIC and income taxes carried out in EUROMOD. Table A3.3 shows that
the number of **Employee Social Insurance Contributions (ils_sicee)**,
the baseline slightly overestimates the number of contributors relative
to external sources, with ratios declining from 1.09 in 2022 to 1.03 in
2024, indicating a gradual convergence but still a persistent upward
bias of around 3–9%. Table A3.4 reveals the amounts for **Employee
social insurance contributions**, Base/Ext ratios of **1.87 in 2022**
and around **1.80 in 2023–2024** imply that baseline contribution
amounts exceed external figures by roughly 80–90%.

A similar but somewhat stronger pattern is observed for the number of
**Employer Social Insurance Contributions (ils_sicer)**, as ca be seen
in able A3.3 where Base/External ratios range from 1.19 in 2022 to 1.13
in 2024, suggesting a consistent overcounting of contributing employers
or jobs in the baseline. The amounts of **Employer Social Insurance
Contributions (ils_sicer)** are overestimated by the model in comparison
to the external figure, with Base/Ext ratios declining slightly from
1.46 in 2022 to about 1.39–1.40 in 2023–2024, corresponding to an
overestimation of around 40%, as can be seen at Table A3.4.

The largest discrepancies emerge for the number of **Self-employed
Social Insurance Contributions (ils_sicse)**, where the Euromod baseline
scenario substantially overestimates the number of contributors: the
Base/External ratio rises from 1.91 in 2022 to 2.55 in 2023 and remains
very high at 2.54 in 2024, implying that the modelled number of
self-employed contributors is more than double the externally observed
figures. Table A3.4 shows, the divergence is even stronger for the
amounts of **Self-employed Social Insurance Contributions (ils_sicse)**,
where the baseline modelled scenario amounts are almost three to four
times higher than external values, with ratios increasing from 2.90 in
2022 to 3.61 in 2023 and remaining elevated at 3.58 in 2024.This could
be due to mis- reporting of self-employment income to the tax
authorities – behaviour which we do not model in EUROMOD. This is
especially valid for the rural areas where thousands of self-employed in
family farming (i.e. without any labour contract) are not registered at
NRA for social insurance purposes.

An even stronger mismatch is observed for the number of **Employer 2
(credited) contributions (ils_sicct),** where ratios of 2.48 in 2022,
4.75 in 2023, and 4.29 in 2024 point to a severe overestimation in the
baseline scenario of the Euromod model (see Table A3.3). The amounts of
**Employer 2 (credited) contributions** show a smaller but rising
discrepancy, with ratios increasing from 1.33 in 2022 to 1.38 in 2024,
revealed in Table A3.4.

According to the legislation, **income tax** is calculated after
employees and self-employed SIC are subtracted from gross income. Thus,
the quality of income tax simulation depends to a large extent on the
accuracy of the SIC simulations. We could not find any official data on
the number of taxpayers in the period 2022-2024 The aggregate amount of
income taxes (tin_s) simulated in EUROMOD slightly underestimates the
external sources (NSI, Government Finance Statistics), about 5–7% under
external values, reporting a baseline/external ratio of 0.94 for 2022,
0.93 in 2023 and 0.95 in 2024 (see Table A3.4). Nevertheless, given the
issues with employment and self-employment incomes reported in EU-SILC
and the simulations of respective SIC, the results on the income tax
simulation should be treated with similar caution.

Overall, these ratios indicate that while employee and employer SIC
payer counts are relatively close to external benchmarks, the baseline
significantly overstates participation among the self-employed and
credited contribution schemes, highlighting priority areas for
calibration and alignment with administrative data. While income tax
amounts are reasonably well calibrated, the baseline substantially
overstates social insurance contribution amounts—particularly for the
self-employed—suggesting differences in contribution bases, rates, or
coverage that warrant recalibration against external fiscal data.

### Validation of benefits

In this subsection, we validate the results from the policy simulations
of benefits carried out in EUROMOD. Table A3.5 shows the number of
benefit recipients and Table A3.6 the average amount of benefits,
according to EUROMOD simulations and external sources.

Regarding the main types of **pensions recipients** (old-age,
disability, and survivor), Table A3.5 and Table A3.6 show that both the
total number of pension recipients and the number and amounts of
Pensions (ils_pen) identified in EU-SILC are close to the external
statistics, with Baseline/External ratios declining from 1.05 in 2022 to
1.03 in 2024 for the number of pensions, indicating a mild
overestimation of total pension coverage that gradually narrows over
time. At the aggregate level, total pension spending in the baseline
exceeds external statistics throughout the period, with Base/External
ratios of 1.09 in 2022, 1.25 in 2023, and 1.24 in 2024, indicating a
growing overestimation of total pension expenditures relative to
observed data (see Table A3.6).

Looking at the different types of pensions, in 2022 EU-SILC
overestimates the number of **Old-age pension (poa00)** recipients by 16
pp compared to the external data, by 15 pp in 2023 and 2024 (see Table
A3.5). Old-age pensions dominate expenditures and are very closely
aligned with input data by construction, but remain moderately above
external figures, with Base/External ratios rising from 1.02 to 1.18
between 2022 and 2024 (see Table A3.6). In contrast, social old-age
pensions (poamt_s) are clearly underestimated, with Base/External ratios
falling from 0.73 in 2022 to 0.35 in 2024, pointing to gaps in input
data of the model (see Table A3.5). Social old-age pensions are
substantially underestimated in monetary terms as well: baseline amounts
represent only 58% of external values in 2022, falling to around 36–38%
in 2023–2024, pointing to a systematic underrepresentation of this
benefit (see Table A3.6). Nevertheless, it should be noted that these
numbers need to be treated with caution due to the small sample size of
entitled individuals in the underlying SILC data.

The number of recipients of Main disability pensions (pdi00) is
underestimated in SILC by 26 pp in 2022, by 28 pp in 2023 and by 32 pp
in 2024; however, the number of Survivor pensions (psu) receivers is
slightly underestimated in EU-SILC (by just 2 pp on average in the
2022-2024 period) in comparison with the external source. These results
suggest that individuals may be misreporting frequently the disability
pensions, merging its amount to the old-age pensions. Survivor pensions
in monetary terms are slightly underestimated but converge toward
external data over time (ratios increasing from 0.85 to 0.96) (see Table
A.3.6). Disability pensions show mixed performance: amounts are below
external values in 2022 (0.86) but slightly above or close to parity in
2023–2024 (1.08 and 1.02) (see Table A.3.6).

As different types of pensions are paid jointly to recipients (either
via a bank transfer or by post), it is possible that recipients do not
distinguish the exact amounts of the different pensions they receive and
thus, they may misclassify pensions in EU-SILC. This likely explains the
discrepancies between the EU-SILC and external statistics for the
separate pension components while the total number/ amount of all
pensions is close to the external figures.

It should be noted that according to the legislation rules entitlements
to family or social assistance benefits – which are simulated in EUROMOD
– are *based on the sum of all pensions*. Therefore, we can expect that
misclassification of pensions across the different types does not pose
an issue for the simulation of these benefits.

The modelling of means-tested benefits shows large heterogeneity across
different type of benefits. Some benefits, such as the number of
**Means-tested child benefits** **(bchmt00_s)**, are reasonably aligned
with external data, with Base/External ratios close to one in earlier
years but drifting downward by 2024, as can be seen in Table A 3.5.
**Means-tested child benefits (bchmt00_s),** in monetary terms, are
closer to external benchmarks but still tend to be overestimated, with
Base/External ratios ranging between 1.1 and 1.5 (see Table A.3.6).

In contrast, the number of monthly **Social assistance allowance
benefits (bsa00_s)** are strongly overestimated, with Base/External
ratios exceeding 4 in 2022 and rising above 5 in 2023, before partially
correcting to 1.83 the external figure in 2024. Spending on social
assistance allowance is heavily overstated as well, with baseline
amounts exceeding external data by factors of 3.5 in 2022, 7.3 in 2023,
and 3.2 in 2024, reflecting either overly broad eligibility or excessive
modelled benefit levels or misreported benefits by respondents.

**Targeted heating allowances (bsaht_s)** show substantial
inconsistencies, with baseline recipient numbers far below external data
in 2022 (ratio 0.52) and remaining understated thereafter, despite large
year-to-year volatility relative to input data, as revealed in Table
A.3.5. Targeted heating allowances are also underestimated in monetary
terms, with Base/External ratios around 0.5–0.8, despite large
year-to-year volatility relative to input data (see Table A.3.6).

The simulation results for the two main social assistance benefits – the
**monthly social assistance allowance** (guaranteed minimum income:
bsa00_s) and the **heating allowance** (bsaht_s) – deviate from the
external data. This could be due to various reasons:

- In the presence of benefit non take-up (see Tasseva, 2016), the number
  of benefit entitlements and the total benefit amounts will be
  oversimulated in EUROMOD as the model assumes full take-up.

- Oversimulation could also occur when incomes, which are used in the
  benefit income-tests and determine benefit entitlements, are
  underreported in EU-SILC.

- Due to lack of information in EU-SILC on assets ownership, the
  asset-test for these benefits is not simulated. As a result, benefit
  entitlements could be oversimulated.

- In EUROMOD we have also assumed the maximum length of benefit receipt:
  12 months for the monthly social assistance allowance and 5 months for
  the heating allowance. However, benefits may have been received for
  shorter periods of time. For example, the heating allowance is
  provided for the heating season which includes only the third quarter
  of year “t” and first quarter of year “t+1”. For more details on
  issues related to the targeting efficiency of the benefits (i.e.
  benefit non take-up and leakage) and data issues, see Tasseva (2016).

**The Benefit for students in 1st grade (bchedyc_s)** shows a consistent
overestimation in both the number of beneficiaries and total amounts
relative to external data. The number of benefits reports a
Base/External ratio of 1.74 (2022), 1.36 (2023) and 1.35 (2024), as
revealed in Table A.3.5. In monetary terms, baseline annual amounts
exceed external values in all observed years, with Base/External ratios
rising from about 1.9 in 2022 to around 2.4 in 2023, before easing to
about 1.5 in 2024, indicating that simulated spending is between 50% and
140% higher than reported aggregates (see Table A.3.6).

Table A.3.5 reveals that **Non-contributory benefits for raising a child
under one year (bmaprnc_s)** are markedly understated, with baseline
amounts covering only 35–50% of external totals, while the monetary
value of this benefit is represented in the range of 20-40%, as can be
seen in Table A.3.6.

The number of **recipients of sickness benefits (bhl)** in EU-SILC is
higher compared to external data but a distinct upward trend for the
discrepancy is underway (in EU-SILC the number of benefit receipts is 14
pp higher than the number from the official NSSI data for 2022).
However, compared to the external figure, the aggregate amount of
sickness benefits in EU-SILC is higher in 2023 by 41 pp and by 24 pp in
2024. The discrepancy between the EU-SILC and the external figure could
be due to possible information mismatches and occasional misreporting.
EU-SILC data of paid sickness benefits overstates external data by 39 pp
in 2022, by 43 pp in 2023 and by 22 pp in 2024.

The **contributory Unemployment benefit** (labelled in EUROMOD as
**bunct_s**) is partially simulated – i.e. the entitlement is
conditioned on the derived benefit receipt in EU-SILC (EUROMOD variable
bunct). Thus, the number of recipients based on EUROMOD are well in line
with the figures based on EU-SILC data and total benefit amount in
EUROMOD is 2 pp lower on average for the 2022-2025 period compared with
EU-SILC data. However, compared to the external figures the number of
benefit recipients is oversimulated in EUROMOD (1.7-3.32 times for the
2022 -2024 period, as shown in Table A3.5). On the other hand, the total
benefit amount simulated in EUROMOD is a ratio of 0.91 on average of the
external figure for 2021-2024 (Table A3.6). The discrepancy between
EUROMOD and the external figures is therefore mainly due to: (i)
imprecise identification of recipients of the unemployment benefit
(bunct) in the SILC sample; and (ii) undersimulated average benefit
amount with EUROMOD (see for more details Section 3.3.3).

Similar to bunct_s, the simulation of **contributory benefit for
pregnancy and childbirth** (bmaprct_s) is partial, i.e. it is
conditioned on the derived benefit receipt in EU-SILC survey (EUROMOD
variable bmaprct). However, as Table A3.5 shows the derived variable
includes only 103 thousand benefit recipients in 2022 (according to
EU-SILC data). The 2022 simulated variable is having a meaning or 28
thousand. This number is much lower than the external figure of 102
thousand benefit recipients in 2021. The aggregate amount of this
benefit simulated in EUROMOD is far from the official NSSI source – BGN
278 million based on EUROMOD simulations versus BGN 555 million
according to the input figure for 2022. Simulated EUROMOD data
understates 2022-2025 values on average by 38 pp, while the baseline
model data understates the external source data by 50 pp in 2022, by 16
pp 2023 and by 14 pp in 2024. These discrepancies can be at least partly
explained by: (i) assumed number of months different from the actual;
(ii) monthly amount estimated on over-assessed contributory income base.

The contributory maternity benefit for bringing up a child up to age 2
(bmaycct_s) shows a systematic underestimation in the baseline model,
evident in both amount and number-related ratios, though with different
magnitudes. In terms of annual amounts, baseline values remain well
below input and external data across all years. The Base/Input ratios
for the amounts’ variables decline from 0.56 in 2022 to 0.47 in 2023,
0.42 in 2024, and 0.38 in 2025, indicating a growing gap between
simulated and input data (see Table A.3.6). Comparison with external
aggregates indicates much closer assessment, with: Base/External ratios
of 0.91 in 2022, 0.92 in 2023, and 0.95 in 2024, suggesting that while
totals are closer to external data than to input values, the baseline
still understates actual expenditures by around 5–9%. Regarding numbers
of beneficiaries, the relatively low Base/External ratios imply that the
model broadly understates participation levels correctly (see Table
A.3.5). The ratio stood at a value of 0.36 in 2022, 0.67 in 2023 and
0.69 in 2024. The number of recipients may be undersimulated because
EU-SILC somewhat underestimates the number of children aged 1 to 4: the
size of that population group in the sample is somewhat lower as
compared to the official data for the population structure in
Bulgaria.[^4]

Compared to the official source, the number of recipients of **birth
grant** (bchbals_s) is understated by 45 pp in 2022 and the total
spending on the **birth grant** (bchbals_s) is understated by 47 pp in
2022. This is at least partly due to underrepresentation of newborns in
EU-SILC input data. On average, 54 pp is the meaning of the
underestimated discrepancy for the 2023-2024 figures of number **birth
grants**(bchbals_s) and 61 pp for the amounts.

Recipients of the **non-means-tested child benefit for mothers in
tertiary education** (bchnm01_s) are occasionally found in the sample,
however the simulation of this benefit is too distant from external data
values, being greatly overersimulated on average external values are
8.67 times higher than the model data, for the 2022-2024 period. Amounts
received are oversimulated by 14.45 times on average, for the
corresponding period. The opposite is valid for the **non-means-tested
child benefit for twins** (bchnm02_s), with the number of benefits being
undersimulated by 66 pp on average for the 2022-2025 period, and amounts
being undersimulated by 36 pp, respectively.

The non-means-tested **benefit for students in** 1<sup>st</sup>,
2<sup>nd</sup>, 3<sup>rd</sup> or 4<sup>th</sup> grade (1<sup>st</sup>
grade – until 1 January 2023) **(bchedyc_s)** and the benefit for
students in 8<sup>th</sup> grade are oversimulated for the number of
benefit recipients. The overestimation for the benefit for students in
1<sup>st</sup> grade is oversimulated by 74 and 36 pp in 2022 and 2023,
and by 39 pp in 2024. **The benefit for students in 8<sup>th</sup> grade
(bchednm_s)** is oversimulated by 178 pp in 2022, by 190 pp in 2023 and
by 177 pp in 2024 respectively. Also, amounts spent on the benefit for
students in 8<sup>th</sup> grade are oversimulated by much, between 185
pp and 188 pp, for the 2022-2024 period**.** As the SILC data does not
contain information on the student’s grade, we try to identify eligible
children by their age and being enrolled in primary school (dec=2). It
is thus likely we oversimulate the number of benefit recipients as some
of the children aged 11 will be already in 5<sup>th</sup> grade. The
situation is similar for the students in the 8<sup>th</sup> grade. Other
possible reasons for oversimulations could be the non-take up or the
fact that the benefit is paid only for the children in the state school
system, but not for those in private schools.

### Validation of outputted (simulated) expenses

The validation of simulated expenditures used to model consumption taxes
includes two types of comparisons:  

1.  Simulated household consumption expenditures compared to
    expenditures collected by National Accounts (NA) of that same
    year.  

<!-- -->

5.  Simulated consumption taxes (based on NA-adjusted simulated
    expenditures) compared to administrative data on consumption tax
    revenues.

Table A3.9 and A3.10 show the validation of consumption taxes related
amounts over the 2022-2025 period. The top part of table A3.9 compares
expenditures aggregated amount from EUROMOD simulations with National
Account (NA) external statistics as reported by EUROSTAT. Coicop level 1
categories perform quite good when looking at “01 – food and
non-alcoholic beverages”, “03 – Clothing and footwear”, “04 – housing,
water and fuel (exc. Imputed rent)” performs quite well and results in
good coverage of relative NA years. Coicop level 1 categories “05 -
Furnishings, household equipment”, “06 – health”, “08 – communications”,
“09 – recreation and culture” also performs relative good as they cover
between 60 and 80% of the NA expenses. The remaining categories instead
are undersimulated and this is probably due to under-reporting of those
categories in original data (e.g. tobacco and alcohol). To note that “08
– communications” is quite oversimulated.

**In food and non-alcoholic beverages (il_x01),** the baseline is close
to external data in 2022, with a model/external ratio of 0.91 and
exceeds it, thereafter, reaching 1.07 in 2023, 1.12 in 2024, and 1.24 in
2025, suggesting an increasing overestimation of food-related
consumption.

**Alcoholic beverages and tobacco (il_x02)** are consistently
underestimated, however, the gap narrows over time, with Base/Ext ratio
rising from 0.73 in 2022 to 0.92 in 2025.

**Clothing and footwear (il_x03)** are well captured overall, having
Base/Ext ratios near parity in 2022 and 2023, with ratios of 0.91 and
0.97, respectively, and is mildly overestimated in 2024 and 2025, with
ratios of 1.02 and 1.12, respectively.

For **Housing, water, and fuels (excluding imputed rent) (il_x04),** the
baseline increasingly overestimates expenditures, with ratios climbing
from 1.02 in 2022 to 1.40 in 2025, indicating a strong upward bias.

By contrast, **Furnishings and household equipment (il_x05)**, are
substantially underestimated throughout, with ratios between 0.52 and
0.69, while **Health expenditure (il_x06)** is also underestimated,
though improving over time, having Base/External ratios from 0.60 to
0.82.

The largest shortfalls are observed in **Transport (il_x07)**, where
Base/External ratios remain very low despite gradual improvement, with
value of **0.42 in 2022** to value of **0.55 in 2025**, pointing to a
major underrepresentation of transport-related consumption.

**Communications** **(il_x08)**, **Recreation and culture (il_x09)**,
**Hotels and restaurants** **(il_x11)**, and **Miscellaneous goods and
services** **(il_x12)** expenditures all show persistent
underestimation, with ratios mostly in the **0.60-0.75 range**, though
trends are upward over time.

**Education (il_x10) expenditures** are the most underestimated
category, with Base/External ratios of around 0.35, reflecting very
limited modelled private education spending.

Total **VAT revenue** **(il_tva)** is consistently underestimated, with
Base/External ratios of **0.64 in 2022**, **0.70 in 2023**, and **0.69
in 2024**, indicating that the non-calibrated consumption base does not
generate sufficient VAT relative to observed revenues.

Similarly, **total excise revenues** **(il_tx)** are underestimated,
with Base/External ratios increasing from **0.42** to **0.52** over
between 2022 and 2024, though remaining far below unity.

At a detailed level, **Spirits (il_tx0211)** and **Beer**
**(il_tx0213)** excises Base/External ratios are severely underestimated
(ratios around **0.14-0.16** for spirits and **0.31-0.41** for beer).
**Tobacco excises** **(il_tx022)** perform better but still fall short,
with ratios rising from **0.53** to **0.61**. Still Wine (il_tx02121)
and Sparkling Wine (il_tx02122) excises are having a Base/Ext ratio of
**0.00, with the model being unable to recognize such excises.**

**Energy (electricity, natural gas, coal-coke ) excises (il_tx045)** are
strongly underestimated, with Base/External ratios ranging between
**0.33 and 0.44.** , excises on **Electricity (il_tx0451)** and
**Natural gas (il_tx04521)** are entirely missing in the baseline
(ratios of **0.00**), and excises on broader energy products show
volatility, including a sharp underestimation in 2024.

Table A3.10 shows that calibration substantially improves the alignment
of indirect tax revenues with external data, but some gaps remain,
especially for excises by product and energy-related taxes.

For VAT, calibration raises revenues markedly compared with the
non-calibrated case, yet the baseline still underestimates observed VAT
receipts. The Base/External ratio declines from 0.89 in 2022 to 0.87 in
2023 and further to 0.81 in 2024.

Total excise revenues also improve after calibration but remain below
external values, with ratios of 0.71 in 2022, 0.77 in 2023, and 0.76 in
2024. This suggests partial correction of the earlier underestimation,
but not full reconciliation with external administrative data.

At a detailed level, spirits excise revenues remain severely
underestimated even after calibration, with ratios stable around 0.20.

Beer excises show moderate improvement relative to the non-calibrated
case but still fall short, with ratios increasing from 0.43 in 2022 to
0.49 in 2024.

Tobacco excises are the best-performing excise category after
calibration, with ratios around 0.73–0.75, though they still imply a
persistent shortfall of roughly one quarter relative to external data.

Energy excises (electricity, gas, coal) remain problematic. Aggregate
energy excise ratios deteriorate sharply in 2024 (0.22), and excises on
electricity and natural gas continue to be entirely absent in the
baseline (ratios of 0.00), despite their presence in external
statistics.

By contrast, total energy-related excises show a strong improvement,
with ratios rising from 0.76 in 2022 to 0.88 in 2024, indicating that
calibration works better at an aggregated level than for individual
energy products.

## Income distribution 

Table A.3.7 and Table A.3.8 present the distributional results based on
equivalised HDI. HDI is the sum of market incomes, non-simulated
pensions and benefits, and simulated benefits minus the non-simulated
property tax, simulated income tax and SIC paid by employees and
self-employed. HDI is equivalised by the “modified OECD” equivalence
scale which assigns the following weights to the household members: 1 to
the household head; 0.5 to any other household member aged 14+; 0.3 to
any other household member aged below 14.

### Income inequality

Table A.3.7 shows results on several income indicators estimated on the
basis of equivalised HDI calculated with EUROMOD which are compared with
Eurostat estimates calculated based on the reported incomes in EU-SILC
data (referred to as the external source). Table A3.7 shows that the
baseline model reproduces the distribution of equivalised disposable
income in Bulgaria reasonably well, especially in 2022 and 2023, with
only modest deviations from external statistics. At the bottom of the
distribution, the first decile is slightly overestimated in 2022
(Base/External ratio of 1.01) and more noticeably in 2023 (1.16),
suggesting that incomes of the poorest households are simulated somewhat
higher than observed. For deciles D2 to D4, baseline shares are very
close to external values, with ratios mostly between 0.92 and 1.03,
indicating a good fit for the lower-middle part of the distribution. In
the middle of the distributions (D5–D7), the baseline aligns almost
perfectly with external data in both years. Ratios are essentially equal
to one, implying that median households are well captured by the model.
This is also reflected in the median equivalized disposable income,
which matches external data almost exactly in 2022 and is moderately
overestimated in 2023 (ratio 1.08), consistent with stronger simulated
income growth.

At the upper end of the distribution, deciles D8 and D9 are slightly
overestimated in 2022, while decile D10 is very close to external data
in both years (ratios of 1.00 in 2022 and 0.97 in 2023). This indicates
that top incomes are broadly well represented, with only a mild
underestimation in 2023.

The discrepancy is mainly driven by the oversimulation of the main
means-tested benefits in EUROMOD (the means-tested child benefit,
monthly social assistance and heating allowances, see Table A.3.5 and
Table A3.6). The median and mean equivalised HDI obtained from EUROMOD
simulations for 2023 overstate the external sources by 8 and 6 pp,
respectively. However, in 2022 EUROMOD results almost perfectly match
the median income and underestimate the mean income by 2 pp,
respectively.

Regarding inequality measures, the Gini coefficient is slightly
overestimated in 2022, with a meaning of 1.02, implying marginally
higher simulated inequality, while in 2023 it is underestimated, with a
value of 0.96, indicating a somewhat more equal distribution than in
external statistics. A similar pattern is observed for the income
quintile share ratio S80/S20, based on EUROMOD-estimated equivalised
HDI, which is higher than external data in 2022, with a meaning of 1.05
and lower in 2023, having a value of 0.90, pointing to year-specific
differences rather than a persistent structural bias.

###  Poverty rates 

Table A.3.8 shows the poverty rates based on different poverty
thresholds, calculated as a percentage of the median HDI. Results based
on EUROMOD simulations are compared with data based on the external
source (Eurostat, EU-SILC). Table A3.8 indicates that the Base/External
ratios for at-risk-of-poverty rates in 2022 and 2023 are generally close
to unity, but with larger and more systematic deviations than those
observed for the income distribution, particularly across poverty
thresholds and demographic groups.

Analysing the 40% of median HDI threshold, ratios in 2022 are very close
to one for all sex groups (1.02 for total, 0.99 for males, and 1.04 for
females), indicating an excellent match with external data. In 2023, all
ratios fall below unity (around 0.88–0.91), suggesting that the baseline
underestimates very-low-income poverty for both men and women.

The 50% threshold signals discrepancies are more pronounced in 2022. The
total population base/external ratio reaches 1.20, driven mainly by a
strong overestimation among females (1.47), while males are slightly
underestimated (0.95). This implies that the baseline assigns
substantially higher poverty rates to women at this threshold than
observed in external data. In 2023, ratios for all sex groups converge
to around 0.97, indicating a much closer alignment.

For the standard 60% of median HDI threshold, base/external ratios in
2022 indicate moderate overestimation, with a meaning of 1.08, again
more pronounced for females, having a value of 1.18 than for males, with
a meaning of 0.97. In 2023, the baseline scenario slightly
underestimates poverty for both sexes, having ratios of about 0.95,
suggesting an improvement in balance but a mild downward bias.

At the 70% threshold, ratios are close to unity in both years. In 2022,
total and female poverty rates are slightly overestimated (1.02 and
1.07), while male rates are marginally underestimated (0.97). In 2023,
ratios hover tightly around 0.97-0.98, pointing to a very good match at
higher relative poverty thresholds.

By age group at the 60% threshold, the baseline performs well for most
groups. In 2022, ratios for the age groups (0-15), (16-24), and (25-49)
are very close to one (0.95-1.01). However, poverty among those aged
50–64 is systematically underestimated in both 2022 and 2023, with a
ratio 0.89 in both years. The largest discrepancy is observed for the
65+ population, where poverty is strongly overestimated in 2022 (1.43)
but underestimated in 2023 (0.91), indicating substantial year-to-year
instability for older-age poverty.

Overall, the ratios for 2022 and 2023 show that the Euromod model
captures broad poverty patterns well, especially at higher thresholds
and for most age groups, but tends to overestimate poverty at
intermediate thresholds (50-60%) for women in 2022 and underestimate
poverty at very low thresholds and among older working-age groups. The
convergence of ratios toward unity in 2023 suggests improved alignment
with external data, though some demographic-specific biases remain.

## Summary of “health warnings”

This summary provides an overview of some of the main issues to be taken
into account when using the Bulgarian component of EUROMOD with income
simulations for 2022-2025:

- Information on investment income in EU-SILC is found to be unreliable.

- Despite our efforts to disaggregate the EU-SILC benefit variables into
  separate benefit components, our derivations do not score well
  compared to the respective national aggregates. This result poses a
  bias for EUROMOD-based estimates of the income distribution. First,
  the bias occurs through the inaccurate derivations of benefits which
  are directly included in household net income – bfamtot, bfanmot,
  bunot, bsaot, bsacm. Second, the quality of partially simulated
  benefits – those conditioned on the derived benefit values – is
  worsened: this is the case with bmaprct_s, bmaycct_s and bunct_s which
  are simulated based on the derived benefits bmaprct, bmaycct and
  bunct, respectively.

- Children aged 0 are underrepresented in EU-SILC. We suspect that this
  is one of the main reasons why some of the major benefits targeted at
  children aged 0 (the birth grant and the non-contributory benefit for
  raising a child under the age of 1) are undersimulated in EUROMOD.

- Although to a lesser extent, the number of children aged 1-4 is
  somewhat underestimated in EU-SILC. In addition to issues with the
  benefit imputations as stated above, we think that this is another
  reason for the undersimulation of the contributory maternity benefit
  for bringing up a child up the age of 2.

- Social assistance benefits – the monthly social assistance allowance
  and heating allowance – are oversimulated in EUROMOD. “The usual
  suspect” for the explanation of this result is the benefit
  non-take-up. Currently, EUROMOD assumes full benefit take-up.

- EUROMOD applies an adjustment for tax non-compliance. It should be
  noted that the aim of the adjustment is simply to calibrate the
  results. Due to lack of data the adjustment cannot reproduce the
  actual tax non-compliance behaviour of individuals and households.

- SIC by employers, employees and self-employed are oversimulated in
  EUROMOD due to issues with reporting of employment and self-employment
  incomes in EU-SILC and the presence of shadow economy. The results on
  the income tax simulation should be also treated with caution.

- The simulation of monetary compensation schemes is triggered by the
  simulation of labour market transitions defined in policy TransLMA_cc.
  This policy becomes operational if the model is run in conjunction
  with the LMA add-on. The nature of these simulations is still
  experimental and only partially validated. Users are encouraged to
  refer to the “*Simulating labour market transitions in EUROMOD*”
  document prior to their use.

- Labour market transitions are switched OFF in EUROMOD baselines. As a
  consequence, the simulation of monetary compensation schemes does not
  produce any effect in baseline simulations. Since all policies not
  linked to labour market transitions are fully functional, it is
  possible for disposable income in 2022 to be higher than disposable
  income in previous years.

## Annex 2. Policy Effects in 2024-2025 

In this section we analyze the direct tax-benefit policy effect on HDI
in Bulgaria between 2024 and 2025. We try to understand how changes (or
non-changes) to tax-benefit policies have affected household incomes in
the period, abstracting from changes in the population characteristics
and the distribution of market/original gross incomes. It should be
noted that tax-benefit policies in a given year are taken as of 30th of
June.

Table A2.1 depicts the policy effect measured in real terms by income
component and income decile group. The effect is estimated as the
difference between simulated household net income under 2024 tax benefit
policies (deflating the tax-benefit monetary parameters by Eurostat’s
Harmonized Index of Consumer Prices, HICP = 1.03633) and net incomes
simulated under 2024 policies, as a percentage of mean equivalized HDI
in 2024. The total policy effect on household income is decomposed into
different components representing the main elements of the national
tax-benefit system.

Table A2.1 shows that in 2024-25 household net income increased on
average by 1.63% in real terms. Across the income distribution,
household net income rose on average for all the deciles in the decile
group as nominal income gains outpaced the growth in prices. The
lowest-income households experienced the highest real gains, with
disposable income rising by 4.31% in the bottom decile and remaining
above 3.25% up to the third decile. Growth then gradually moderates
across the middle of the distribution, falling to around 2.0–2.5% in
deciles four to six, and declines further in the upper-middle deciles.
The top of the income distribution benefits the least, with real
disposable income increasing by only 1.23% in the ninth decile and just
0.39% in the highest decile. The growth rate of the 9<sup>nth</sup> and
especially the 10<sup>th</sup> decile groups is lagging, supporting the
inequality reduction process that is underway. Figure A2.1. visualizes
very well the process of income redistribution, from the upper deciles
to the bottom deciles.

***Public pensions*** appear to be the largest contributing factor to
overall household income increase, in nominal and real terms. Incomes of
bottom decile, namely the bottom half of the distribution, outpace the
growth of upper two deciles, especially for the top decile. The
contribution of public pensions amounts to 1.64 percentage points in the
bottom decile and exceeding 3 percentage points in the second and third
deciles. This contribution gradually diminishes across the middle
deciles, falling to around 2.4–2.9 percentage points in deciles four and
five and to below 2 percentage points from the sixth decile onward. In
the upper part of the distribution, the growth effect becomes much
weaker, reaching only 1.34 percentage points in the ninth decile and
0.63 percentage points in the top decile, ultimately contributing to
more equal income distribution.

Real income growth from means-tested benefits is highly concentrated in
the lower part of the income distribution. The bottom decile records a
very large contribution of 2.68 percentage points to real income growth,
which explains a substantial share of its overall disposable income
increase. This effect drops sharply in the second decile to 0.44
percentage points and becomes marginal from the third decile onward,
falling below 0.1 percentage points by the fourth and fifth deciles.
From the middle of the distribution upward, the contribution of
means-tested benefits is close to zero and even slightly negative in the
seventh decile. Overall, means-tested transfers play a decisive role in
protecting the lowest-income households from real income losses, while
having virtually no impact on income growth for middle- and high-income
groups.

Changes to ***non-means-tested benefits*** led to income gains to
household net incomes, of 0.08% on average, in real terms. The
contribution is very small in the lower deciles, amounting to 0.02
percentage points in the bottom decile, and increases slightly in the
middle of the distribution, reaching around 0.07–0.09 percentage points
in deciles four to six. The effect becomes somewhat stronger in the
upper-middle deciles, peaking at 0.14 percentage points in the seventh
decile, before declining again toward the top, where it falls to 0.04
percentage points in the highest decile. Overall, non-means-tested
benefits act as a weak but stabilizing source of real income growth
across the distribution, with limited redistributive power compared to
means-tested benefits or public pensions. Benefits provided to people
with disabilities were increased by 11.5% for the supplement for care by
assistant (bdi_s) and 4.4% for the monthly financial support for people
with disabilities (pdimmtu_s). However, considering the relatively small
number of recipients of these benefits, these positive developments led
to small increase on average at decile group level.

Changes to ***Employee social insurance contributions (SIC)*** affected
the average household income negatively, declining by -0.13% on average,
in real terms. Upper three decile income group experienced a real income
decline of -0.08% for the 8<sup>th</sup> decile, -0.18% for the
9<sup>nth</sup> decile, and -0.24% for the top decile, respectively.

Changes to ***direct taxes*** led to tiny declines in household net
incomes, of 0.01% on average for the entire population. All decile
groups experience a decline in their real disposable income but the
ninth and tenth decile group. Small income gain, of 0.02% of the real
disposable income due to the direct taxation policy is evident for the
top decile. Income decile groups from the 1<sup>st</sup> to the
6<sup>th</sup> decile reported a real income decline, at a pace of 0.03%
on average.

\* \* \*

To conclude, the policy developments in 2024-25 led to real income
improvements for all decile groups as a result of increased amounts of
public pensions, in particular, and to a much lesser degree, caused by
non-means-tested benefits. The richest decile group is also subject to
the lowest increase of the income from public pension, which led to the
fact that the top decile’s real income rose at the slowest pace, in
comparison to other decile groups. Public pensions constitute the main
and most broadly effective source of real income growth across all
deciles, while means-tested benefits provide strong and targeted
protection to the lowest-income households, explaining the pronounced
pro-poor pattern of income growth. Non-means-tested benefits play only a
minor stabilizing role, contributing modestly and relatively evenly
across the distribution. Taken together, these components result in a
clear compression of real income growth across deciles, with
lower-income groups substantially shielded from inflation and
higher-income households experiencing weak or near-stagnant real income
gains.
